Since the individual annotators do not always agree on the content of each image, we can think of their labels as being a randomly contaminated version of the corresponding “true” labels contained in the original CIFAR-10 data set.
Our goal is to construct informative prediction sets for the true labels, using a conformal predictor calibrated on the contaminated data.
For simplicity, we work with a slightly modified version of the CIFAR-10H data in which each image has a single corrupted label
\tilde{Y}
, randomly sampled from a multinomial distribution whose weights are equal to the relative label frequencies assigned to that image by different human annotators.
Note that these corrupted labels coincide with the true CIFAR-10 labels approximately 95.4% of the time; see Figure
A43
(a) for a visualization of some images for which the labels do not match.

A ResNet-18 convolutional neural network serves as base classifier; this was implemented by the PyTorch Python package
[
52
]
and pre-trained using the 50,000 CIFAR-10 images excluded from the CIFAR-10H data set.
The output of the final soft-max layer of the deep neural network provides estimates of the class probabilities for any new given image, and from that we calculate conformity scores with the recipe reviewed in Section
A1.1
.
The conformal predictor is then calibrated using three alternative methods, based on a random subset of the 10,000 CIFAR-10H images whose size is varied as a control parameter.

Due to the larger number of labels,
K=10
, and to help highlight the flexibility of our methods, we focus first on marginal (
5
) instead of label-conditional (
4
) coverage.
The first method considered is Algorithm
4
, the standard conformal approach that seeks marginal coverage while ignoring label contamination.
The second method,
Adaptive+
, is the optimistic variation of our Algorithm
7
from Section
A6.1
, which we apply imagining that the label contamination process follows the randomized response model described in Section
A3.1
.
This model assumes that the observed labels are conditionally independent of the image features, given the true labels, and that an incorrect label is as likely as any other incorrect label. Despite the potential misalignment with the true data generating process, this model is useful for our purposes.
Specifically, it simplifies the matrix
M
, allowing us to characterize it using a single noise parameter,
\epsilon\in[0,1)
, which is fixed here as
\epsilon=0.051
.
This
\epsilon
value is selected to match the mean fraction of CIFAR-10H samples where
Y\neq\tilde{Y}
, roughly
0.046=\epsilon(1-1/K)
.
The third method,
Adaptive+ (plug-in)
, differs from
Adaptive+
in that it utilizes a plug-in estimate of
\epsilon
obtained via maximum-likelihood.
This estimate is evaluated as explained in Section
A3.1
, by applying the same pre-trained ResNet-18 convolutional neural network to a smaller independent data set containing both clean and corrupted data in equal proportions.
In particular, the number of clean images used to estimate
\epsilon
is 10% of the total number of corrupted calibration images.

Figure
1
, previewed in Section
1.2
, reports on the prediction sets constructed by the three methods for a random test set 500 CIFAR-10H images, varying the size of the calibration sample between 500 and 9500.
All experiments are independently repeated 50 times, using different random splits of the CIFAR-10H data into calibration, model-fitting, and test subsets.
The results show that the standard method is overly conservative, while our adaptive approaches are able to achieve valid coverage with increasingly more informative prediction sets as the size of the calibration sample grows.
See Figure
A43
(b) for a visualization of some concrete examples in which our
Adaptive+
method leads to more informative prediction sets compared to the standard conformal inference benchmark.
Finally, Figure
A44
in Section
A8.5
presents analogous results from similar experiments in which we target label-conditional coverage, using Algorithms
1
and
3
instead of Algorithms
7
and
4
, respectively.
Note that Algorithm
1
requires a larger data set in this case, compared to the marginal coverage setting, in order to produce prediction sets that are significantly more informative compared to those of Algorithm
3
.
The reason for this is the stronger nature of the label-conditional guarantee, which effectively diminishes the usable sample size for both Algorithm
1
and Algorithm
3
by a factor of
K=10
.

5
Discussion

This paper studied in-depth the problem of conformal classification in the presence of calibration data with contaminated labels.
Our research contributes to the growing literature on conformal inference beyond exchangeability
[
20
]
, addressing an open practical problem.
A key innovation of our approach is its capacity to automatically adjust to random label contamination, resulting in prediction sets that offer more robust coverage guarantees and are often more informative compared to those given by state-of-the-art approaches.
Additionally, our framework is highly flexible, enabling several variations of our methodology that target different coverage metrics and can accommodate varying degrees of understanding of the label contamination process.
These adaptations hint at potential for further expansion in future research. Both theoretical and practical findings presented in this paper underscore the enhanced benefits of our adaptive approach when employed with larger sets of contaminated calibration data. This aspect renders our research particularly applicable to real-world situations where there are abundant data of modest quality, but precise labels are scarce.

This research opens several opportunities for future work.
For example, it may be interesting to study possible extensions of our methods that can be applied with regression data, or even with other types of more complex data for which conformal inference has already been utilized, including causal inference
[
53
]
, survival analysis
[
54
]
, and matrix completion
[
55
]
.
Alternatively, it may be possible to account for label contamination in the context of more sophisticated conformal prediction frameworks such as full-conformal inference
[
1
]
and cross-validation+
[
56
]
, which are more computationally expensive but can make more efficient use of limited observations.
Finally, future research might be able to uncover further methodological opportunities by combining the ideas presented in this paper with the theoretical analyses of
[
20
]
.

A software implementation of the methods presented in this paper is available online at
https://github.com/msesia/conformal-label-noise
.

Appendix A1
Review of standard conformal classification methods

A1.1
Conformity scores based on generalized inverse quantiles

We briefly review here the construction of the generalized inverse quantile conformity scores proposed by
[
40
]
, upon which we rely in the empirical demonstrations of Section
4
.
These conformity scores are more involved compared to the classical homogeneous scores defined in (
2
), but they have the advantage of leading to more flexible prediction sets that can account for possible heteroscedasticity in the distribution of
Y\mid X
. We refer to
[
40
]
or
[
39
]
for further information about the limitations of the scores in (
2
).

For any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
t\in[0,1]
, define

\displaystyle\hat{Q}(x,\hat{\pi},t)

\displaystyle=\min\{k\in\{1,\ldots,K\}:\hat{\pi}_{(1)}(x)+\hat{\pi}_{(2)}(x)+\ldots+\hat{\pi}_{(k)}(x)\geq t\},

(A33)

where
\hat{\pi}_{(1)}(x)\geq\ldots\geq\hat{\pi}_{(K)}(x)
are the descending order statistics of
\hat{\pi}(x,1),\ldots,\hat{\pi}(x,K)
.
Intuitively,
\hat{Q}(x,\hat{\pi},\cdot)
may be seen as a generalized quantile function.
Similarly, let
\hat{r}(x,\hat{\pi},k)
denote the rank of
\hat{\pi}(x,k)
among
\hat{\pi}(x,1),\ldots,\hat{\pi}(x,K)
.
With this notation, one can also define a corresponding generalized cumulative distribution function:

\displaystyle\hat{\Pi}(x,\hat{\pi},k)=\hat{\pi}_{(1)}(x)+\hat{\pi}_{(2)}(x)+\ldots+\hat{\pi}_{(\hat{r}(x,\hat{\pi},k))}(x).

Then, the function
\mathcal{C}
proposed by
[
40
]
can be written as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(x;\hat{\pi},\tau)

\displaystyle=\{k\in[K]:\hat{r}(x,\hat{\pi},k)\leq\hat{Q}(x,\hat{\pi},\tau_{k})\},

(A34)

and the corresponding conformity scores defined in (
1
) can be evaluated efficiently by noting that
\hat{s}(x,k)=\hat{\Pi}(x,k)
; see
[
40
]
for further details.

The prediction function defined in (
A34
) may be understood by noting that, if
\tau=(\tau_{0},\ldots,\tau_{0})
for some
\tau_{0}\in[0,1]
, the output of
\mathcal{C}(x;\hat{\pi},\tau)
is the list of most likely classes according to
\hat{\pi}(x)
up until the first label
l
for which
\hat{\Pi}(x,\hat{\pi},l)\geq\tau_{0}
.
Therefore, in the ideal case where
\hat{\pi}(x,k)=\mathbb{P}\left[Y=k\mid X=x\right]
, one can verify that
\mathcal{C}(x;\hat{\pi},\tau)
is the smallest possible (deterministic) prediction set for
Y
with perfect object-conditional coverage at level
\tau_{0}
, i.e., satisfying
\mathbb{P}\left[Y\in\mathcal{C}(x;\hat{\pi},\tau)\mid X=x\right]\geq\tau_{0}
.
Note that
[
40
]
also developed a more powerful randomized version of (
A34
) that enjoys similar theoretical properties while being able to produce even more informative prediction sets.
The results of this paper can also seamlessly accommodate such additional randomness in
\mathcal{C}
, and indeed that is the practical approach followed in the empirical demonstrations of Section
4
, but we choose not to review such extension explicitly here to avoid making the notation too cumbersome.

A1.2
Prediction sets with label-conditional coverage

1

Input:

Data set
\{(X_{i},\tilde{Y}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
, with observable labels
\tilde{Y}_{i}\in[K]
.

Unlabeled test point with features
X_{n+1}
.

Machine learning algorithm
\mathcal{A}
for training a
K
-class classifier.

Prediction function
\mathcal{C}
satisfying Definition
1
; e.g., (
2
).

Desired coverage parameter
\alpha
.

Randomly split
[n]
into two disjoint subsets,
\mathcal{D}^{\text{train}}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{cal}}
.

2

Train the classifier
\mathcal{A}
on the data in
\mathcal{D}^{\text{train}}
.

3

for

k=1,\dots,K

do

4

Define
\mathcal{D}_{k}^{\mathrm{cal}}=\{i\in\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{cal}}:Y_{i}=k\}
and
n_{k}=|\mathcal{D}_{k}^{\mathrm{cal}}|
.

5

Compute
\hat{s}(X_{i},k)
using (
1
), for all
i\in\mathcal{D}_{k}^{\mathrm{cal}}
.

6

Define
\hat{\tau}_{k}
as the
\lceil(1+n_{k})\cdot(1-\alpha)\rceil
smallest value in
\{\hat{s}(X_{i},k)\}_{i\in\mathcal{D}_{k}^{\mathrm{cal}}}
.

7

end for

8

Evaluate
\hat{C}(X_{n+1})=\mathcal{C}(X_{n+1},\hat{\tau};\hat{\pi})
, where
\hat{\tau}=(\hat{\tau}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\tau}_{K})
.

9

Output:
Conformal prediction set
\hat{C}(X_{n+1})
for
Y_{n+1}
, satisfying (
4
).

10

Algorithm 3

Standard conformal classification with label-conditional coverage.

Proposition A1

(e.g., from
[
47
]
or
[
40
]
)
.

If the data pairs
(X_{i},\tilde{Y}_{i})
, for all
i\in[n+1]
, are exchangeable random samples from some joint distribution, the prediction set
\hat{C}(X_{n+1})
output by Algorithm
3
has label-conditional coverage (
4
) for the observable labels
\tilde{Y}
; i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\tilde{Y}_{n+1}\in\hat{C}(X_{n+1})\mid\tilde{Y}_{n+1}=k\right]\geq 1-\alpha,\qquad\text{ for all }k\in[K].

Further, if all scores
\hat{s}(X_{i},\tilde{Y}_{i})
computed by Algorithm
3
are almost-surely distinct,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\tilde{Y}_{n+1}\in\hat{C}(X_{n+1})\mid\tilde{Y}_{n+1}=k\right]\leq 1-\alpha+\frac{1}{n_{k}+1},\qquad\text{ for all }k\in[K],

where
n_{k}=|\mathcal{D}_{k}^{\mathrm{cal}}|
is the number of data points with label
k
in the calibration set.

In words, this result tells us that Algorithm
3
is always guaranteed to achieve valid label-conditional coverage for the observable labels
\tilde{Y}
, regardless of which prediction function
\mathcal{C}
is employed.
Note that in the standard setting without label contamination, the observable labels
\tilde{Y}
are simply assumed to be always equal to
Y
.

A1.3
Prediction sets with marginal coverage

1

Data set
\{(X_{i},\tilde{Y}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
with observable labels
\tilde{Y}_{i}\in[K]
.

Unlabeled test point with features
X_{n+1}
.

Machine learning algorithm
\mathcal{A}
for training a
K
-class classifier.

Prediction function
\mathcal{C}
satisfying Definition
1
; e.g., (
2
).

Desired coverage parameter
\alpha
.

Randomly split
[n]
into two disjoint subsets,
\mathcal{D}^{\text{train}}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{cal}}
, defining
n_{\mathrm{cal}}=|\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{cal}}|
.

2

Train the classifier
\mathcal{A}
on the data in
\mathcal{D}^{\text{train}}
.

3

Compute
\hat{s}(X_{i},\tilde{Y}_{i})
using (
1
), for all
i\in\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{cal}}
.

Define
\hat{\tau}
as the
\lceil(1+n_{\mathrm{cal}})\cdot(1-\alpha)\rceil
smallest value in
\{\hat{s}(X_{i},\tilde{Y}_{i})\}_{i\in\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{cal}}}
.

5

Evaluate
\hat{C}(X_{n+1})=\mathcal{C}(X_{n+1},\hat{\tau};\hat{\pi})
, where
\hat{\tau}=(\hat{\tau}_{0},\ldots,\hat{\tau}_{0})
.

6

Conformal prediction set
\hat{C}(X_{n+1})
for
\tilde{Y}_{n+1}
, satisfying (
5
).

7

Algorithm 4

Standard conformal classification with marginal coverage.

Proposition A2

(e.g., from
[
26
]
or
[
40
]
)
.

If the data pairs
(X_{i},\tilde{Y}_{i})
, for all
i\in[n+1]
, are exchangeable random samples from some joint distribution, the prediction set
\hat{C}(X_{n+1})
output by Algorithm
3
has marginal coverage (
5
) for the observable labels
\tilde{Y}
; i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\tilde{Y}_{n+1}\in\hat{C}(X_{n+1})\right]\geq 1-\alpha.

Further, if all scores
\hat{s}(X_{i},\tilde{Y}_{i})
computed by Algorithm
3
are almost-surely distinct,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\tilde{Y}_{n+1}\in\hat{C}(X_{n+1})\right]\leq 1-\alpha+\frac{1}{n_{\mathrm{cal}}+1},

where
n_{\mathrm{cal}}=|\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{cal}}|
is the number of data points in the calibration set.

Algorithm 6

Adaptive classification with contamination model fitting (plug-in)

Appendix A3
Simplified methods for special contamination models

This section illustrates two specific instances of the general label contamination model introduced in Section
2.4
.
These are particular cases in which the implementation of the adaptive conformal prediction methods developed in this paper can be significantly streamlined, and the estimation of their parameters, as discussed in Section
3.3
, is also simplified.
We begin in Section
A3.1
with an exploration of label contamination processes described by the one-parameter randomized response model
[
11
]
.
Following this, Section
A3.2
expands to consider more complex processes described by a two-parameter extension of the randomized response model, which is specifically designed to accommodate potential label hierarchies.

A3.1
The randomized response model

A3.1.1
Model description

Suppose that the relation between
X,Y
and
\tilde{Y}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\tilde{Y}=k\mid X,Y=l\right]=(1-\epsilon)\mathbb{I}\left[k=l\right]+\frac{\epsilon}{K},

(A35)

for all
l,k\in[K]
, where
\epsilon\in[0,1)
is a scalar parameter controlling the amount of random label
noise
.
This setup corresponds to the classical
randomized response
model of
[
11
, ]
, and it has recently found many relevant applications in the context of differential privacy
[
57
,
58
,
14
]
.
In particular, a well-known technique for achieving
(\varepsilon,0)
-
label differential privacy
consists of replacing each individual observation of
Y
with a noisy label
\tilde{Y}
according to the randomized response model defined in (
A35
), with the parameter
\epsilon
given by

\displaystyle\epsilon=\frac{K}{e^{\varepsilon}+K-1}.

We refer to
[
14
]
for a formal definition of label differential privacy and a proof of this result.

In the notation of Section
2.4
, the randomized response model described above corresponds to a matrix
M
(
10
) with the form

\displaystyle M_{kl}=\frac{(1-\epsilon)\rho_{k}}{(1-\epsilon)\rho_{k}+\epsilon/K}\mathbb{I}\left[k=l\right]+\frac{\rho_{l}\cdot\epsilon/K}{(1-\epsilon)\rho_{k}+\epsilon/K},

(A36)

for any
k,l\in[K]
, while the contaminated label frequencies become

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}_{k}=(1-\epsilon)\rho_{k}+\frac{\epsilon}{K}.

(A37)

Note that Equation (
A36
) follows from (
A35
) with a straightforward application of Bayes’ rule, and then Equation (
A37
) is easily obtained by recalling that
\tilde{\rho}_{k}:=\mathbb{P}[\tilde{Y}=k]
for all
k\in[K]
.

As long as
\epsilon<1
and
\rho_{k}>0
for all
k\in[K]
, this matrix
M
can be inverted analytically by applying the Sherman-Morrison formula, which leads to
V=M^{-1}
with

\displaystyle\begin{split}V_{kl}&=\left(1+\frac{\epsilon}{1-\epsilon}\cdot\frac{1/K}{\rho_{k}}\right)\mathbb{I}\left[k=l\right]-\frac{\epsilon}{(1-\epsilon)K}\cdot\frac{(1-\epsilon)\rho_{l}+\epsilon/K}{\rho_{k}}\\
&=\frac{\tilde{\rho}_{k}}{\tilde{\rho}_{k}-\epsilon/K}\mathbb{I}\left[k=l\right]-\frac{\epsilon}{K}\cdot\frac{\tilde{\rho}_{l}}{\tilde{\rho}_{k}-\epsilon/K}.\end{split}

(A38)

The relatively simple structure of this matrix
V
makes the randomized response model particularly interesting to focus on.
In particular, we will see below how this model leads to an easier-to-interpret version of the general methodology presented in Section
3
.
Further, the scalar nature of the unknown parameter
\epsilon\in[0,1)
simplifies the task of empirically fitting the model given a limited amount of clean data, thus overcoming the main practical limitation of the general estimation approach described in Section
3.3
.

A3.1.2
Adaptive coverage under known label noise

We begin by showing how the adaptive conformal prediction methodology from Section
3.1
simplifies under a randomized response model for the label contamination process.
First, note that the plug-in estimate
\hat{\Delta}_{k}(t)
of the coverage inflation factor
\Delta_{k}(t)
utilized by Algorithm
1
, originally defined in (
14
), simplifies to:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\Delta}_{k}(t)&=\frac{\epsilon(1-\tilde{\rho}_{k})}{K\tilde{\rho}_{k}-\epsilon}\left[\hat{F}_{k}^{k}(t)-\frac{\sum_{l\neq k}\tilde{\rho}_{l}\hat{F}_{l}^{k}(t)}{\sum_{l\neq k}\tilde{\rho}_{l}}\right],\end{split}

(A39)

while the finite-sample correction term
\delta(n_{k},n_{*})
defined in (
18
) simplifies to

\displaystyle\delta(n_{k},n_{*})

\displaystyle=c(n_{k})+\frac{2\epsilon(1-\tilde{\rho}_{k})}{(K\tilde{\rho}_{k}-\epsilon)\sqrt{n_{*}}}\min\left\{K\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}},\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{*}}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(2K)+\log(n_{*})}{2}}\right\}.

(A40)

Similarly, under the randomized response model, Assumption
4
becomes

\displaystyle\inf_{t\in(0,1)}\Delta_{k}(t)\geq-\alpha+c(n_{k})+\frac{2\epsilon(1-\tilde{\rho}_{k})}{(K\tilde{\rho}_{k}-\epsilon)\sqrt{n_{*}}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{*}}}+2\sqrt{\frac{\log(2K)+\log(n_{*})}{2}}\right),

which is always satisfied in the large-sample limit,
n_{*}\to\infty
, as long as

\displaystyle\epsilon\leq\min\left\{K\tilde{\rho}_{k},\frac{\alpha K\tilde{\rho}_{k}}{1+\alpha-\tilde{\rho}_{k}}\right\},

(A41)

regardless of whether the stochastic dominance condition in (
11
) holds.

Combined with Theorem
3
, these expressions tell us that the prediction sets output by Algorithm
1
have asymptotically tight coverage if the noise parameter
\epsilon
is not too large and the regularity conditions of Assumptions
2
–
3
hold.
For example, if
\alpha=0.1
and
\tilde{\rho}_{k}=1/K
for all
k\in[K]
, the upper bound in (
A41
) becomes
\epsilon\leq 0.167
if
K=2
, and
\epsilon\leq 0.111
if
K=5
.

Further, this model makes it easy to bound from above the term
\varphi_{k}(n_{k},n_{*})
in Theorem
3
, with a bound that only increases with
K
at rate
\sqrt{\log K}
. Intuitively, this means that the asymptotic tightness of the prediction sets output by Algorithm
1
also holds for classification problems with many possible classes.

A3.1.3
Adaptive coverage under a bounded label contamination model

The randomized response model also allows simplifying our general method for constructing adaptive prediction sets under imperfect knowledge of the label contamination process.
Since the label frequencies
\tilde{\rho}_{k}
are easy to estimate accurately from the available contaminated data for all
k\in[K]
, the expression for the matrix
V
in (
A38
) involves only one possibly unknown quantity, the scalar noise parameter
\epsilon\in[0,1)
.
In fact, it is easy to verify that, for any
l,k\in[K]
, the matrix entry
V_{kl}
can be equivalently rewritten as

\displaystyle V_{kl}

\displaystyle=\frac{\tilde{\rho}_{k}}{\tilde{\rho}_{k}-\epsilon/K}\mathbb{I}\left[k=l\right]-\frac{\xi\tilde{\rho}_{l}}{K\tilde{\rho}_{k}+\xi(K\tilde{\rho}_{k}-1)},

(A42)

where
\xi:=\epsilon/(1-\epsilon)>0
is a monotone increasing transformation of
\epsilon
.

Therefore, in this special case, implementing the methods from Section
3.2
only requires a valid confidence interval for
\xi
, in lieu of a joint confidence region for all off-diagonal elements of
V
.
In particular, if a confidence interval
[\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{low}},\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{upp}}]
at level
1-\alpha_{V}
is available for
\xi
, then it follows immediately from (
A42
) and (
A37
) that a valid joint confidence region
[\hat{V}^{\mathrm{low}},\hat{V}^{\mathrm{upp}}]
for the off-diagonal elements of
V
is given by:

\displaystyle\hat{V}^{\mathrm{upp}}_{kl}=-\frac{\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{low}}\tilde{\rho}_{l}}{K\tilde{\rho}_{k}+\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{upp}}(K\tilde{\rho}_{k}-1)},

\displaystyle\hat{V}^{\mathrm{low}}_{kl}=-\frac{\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{upp}}\tilde{\rho}_{l}}{K\tilde{\rho}_{k}+\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{low}}(K\tilde{\rho}_{k}-1)},

\displaystyle\forall l\neq k.

(A43)

To simplify the following notation as much as possible, but without much loss of generality, let us make the additional assumption that the noisy label frequencies are uniform:
\tilde{\rho}_{k}=1/K
for all
k\in[K]
. Note that (
A37
) tells us this is always the case if the true labels are uniform.
Then, Equation (
A43
) implies that, for all
l\neq k
,

\displaystyle\hat{V}^{\mathrm{upp}}_{kl}=-\frac{\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{low}}}{K},

\displaystyle\hat{V}^{\mathrm{low}}_{kl}=-\frac{\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{upp}}}{K},

\displaystyle\hat{\delta}^{(V)}_{kl}:=\hat{V}^{\mathrm{upp}}_{kl}-\hat{V}^{\mathrm{low}}_{kl}=\frac{\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{upp}}-\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{low}}}{K},

and therefore
\hat{\delta}^{(V)}_{k*}:=\max_{l\neq k}\hat{\delta}^{(V)}_{kl}=\delta_{\hat{\xi}}/K
, where
\delta_{\hat{\xi}}:=\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{upp}}-\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{low}}
.
Further,
\hat{\zeta}_{k}
from (
20
) is equal to zero because
\hat{V}^{\mathrm{upp}}_{kl}=\hat{V}^{\mathrm{upp}}_{kl^{\prime}}
and
V_{kl}=V_{kl^{\prime}}
for all
l,l^{\prime}\neq k
.
Thus, in this special case, our method from Section
3.2
can be implemented using the following simplified versions of the plug-in estimator
\hat{\Delta}_{k}^{\mathrm{ci}}(t)
defined in (
21
):

\displaystyle\hat{\Delta}_{k}^{\mathrm{ci}}(t)

\displaystyle=\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{low}}\left(1-\frac{1}{K}\right)\left[\hat{F}_{k}^{k}(t)-\frac{\sum_{l\neq k}\hat{F}_{l}^{k}(t)}{K-1}\right]-\delta_{\hat{\xi}}\left(1-\frac{1}{K}\right)\left|\hat{F}_{k}^{k}(t)-\frac{\sum_{l\neq k}\hat{F}_{l}^{k}(t)}{K-1}\right|,

(A44)

and
\delta^{\mathrm{ci}}(n_{k},n_{*})
in (
22
),

\displaystyle\delta^{\mathrm{ci}}(n_{k},n_{*})

\displaystyle=c(n_{k})+\frac{2\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{upp}}(1-1/K)}{\sqrt{n_{*}}}\min\left\{K\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}},\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{*}}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(2K)+\log(n_{*})}{2}}\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad+2\alpha_{V}\bar{\xi}^{\mathrm{upp}}(1-1/K),

where
\bar{\xi}^{\mathrm{upp}}
is a (possibly very conservative) deterministic upper bound on
\xi
.

These simplified expressions highlight that, in principle, Algorithm
2
only requires a one-sided confidence interval
[0,\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{upp}}]
for
\xi
in order to achieve valid coverage, because one could always evaluate
\hat{\Delta}_{k}^{\mathrm{ci}}(t)
in (
A44
) using
\smash{\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{low}}=0}
and
\smash{\delta_{\hat{\xi}}=\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{upp}}}
.
However, we already know from Section
3.2
that the prediction sets computed by Algorithm
2
tend to be more informative if the confidence bounds are tighter.
In particular, the
1-\alpha+\varphi_{k}^{\mathrm{ci}}(n_{k},n_{*})
coverage upper bound given by Theorem
5
can be interpreted even more intuitively in this special case, since

\displaystyle\varphi_{k}^{\mathrm{ci}}(n_{k},n_{*})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n_{*}}+2c(n_{k})+\frac{2}{n_{k}}\cdot\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}\left[1+\epsilon\cdot\frac{f_{\max}}{f_{\min}}\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{n_{k}+1}\frac{1}{j}\right]+\left(1+4\bar{\xi}^{\mathrm{upp}}\right)\alpha_{V}+2\mathbb{E}\left[\delta_{\hat{\xi}}\right]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{4\mathbb{E}\left[\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{upp}}\right]}{\sqrt{n_{*}}}\min\left\{K\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}},\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{*}}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(2K)+\log(n_{*})}{2}}\right\}.

This highlights that the prediction sets output by Algorithm
2
can be (approximately) asymptotically tight, under Assumption
5
, if
\bar{\xi}^{\mathrm{upp}}
is finite,
\alpha_{V}
is small, and the expected
\mathbb{E}[\delta_{\hat{\xi}}]
length of the confidence interval for
\xi
is also small.
Further, under this model, Assumption
5
becomes approximately equivalent, in the limit of
n_{*}\to\infty
, to

\displaystyle\xi+2\delta_{\hat{\xi}}\leq\alpha-2\alpha_{V}\bar{\xi}^{\mathrm{upp}}

(A45)

This means that Assumption
5
is often realistic, similarly to Assumption
4
, as long as
\alpha_{V}\leq\alpha
, the noise parameter
\epsilon
is not too large, and the confidence interval
[\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{low}},\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{upp}}]
is not too wide.
For example, suppose
K=2
,
\alpha=0.1
,
\bar{\xi}^{\mathrm{upp}}=0.125
, and the confidence interval
[\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{low}},\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{upp}}]=[0.075,0.125]
has level
\alpha_{V}=0.01
and width
\delta_{\hat{\xi}}=0.05
.
Then, the upper bound for the true noise parameter
\xi
in (
25
) is
\xi\leq 0.0975
, which corresponds to
\epsilon\leq 0.089
.
Additionally, Assumption
5
is also guaranteed to be satisfied in the large-sample limit if the stochastic dominance condition in (
11
) holds (i.e.,
\inf_{t\in(0,1)}\Delta_{k}(t)\geq 0
) and a sufficiently tight confidence interval
[\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{low}},\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{upp}}]
for a small enough
\alpha_{V}
is available.

A3.1.4
Estimating the parameter
\epsilon

The scalar noise parameter
\epsilon\in[0,1)
in the randomized response model described in Section
A3.1.1
can be empirically estimated, from a small amount of clean data, by applying a simplified version of the general procedure described in Section
3.3
.

Recall that the matrices
\tilde{Q}
and
Q
, defined in (
27
), are such that, for any
l,k\in[K]
,

\displaystyle\tilde{Q}_{kl}:=\mathbb{P}\left[\hat{f}(X)=l\mid\tilde{Y}=k,\hat{f}\right],

\displaystyle Q_{kl}:=\mathbb{P}\left[\hat{f}(X)=l\mid Y=k,\hat{f}\right].

We prove in Section
A7.4
that, under the randomized response model, the estimating equation
\tilde{Q}=MQ
stated in (
28
) implies

\displaystyle\epsilon=\frac{\psi-\tilde{\psi}}{\psi-1/K},

(A46)

where
\tilde{\psi}
and
\psi
are the probabilities that the classifier guesses correctly the corrupted and true label, respectively, of a new independent data point,

\displaystyle\tilde{\psi}:=\mathbb{P}\left[\hat{f}(X)=\tilde{Y}\mid\hat{f}\right],

\displaystyle\psi:=\mathbb{P}\left[\hat{f}(X)=Y\mid\hat{f}\right].

(A47)

This result suggests the following simplified method for estimating
\epsilon
.
The clean data in
\mathcal{D}^{0}
can be used to compute an intuitive empirical estimate of
\psi
:

\displaystyle\psi

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{|\mathcal{D}^{0}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{D}^{0}}\mathbb{I}\left[Y_{i}=\hat{f}(X_{i})\right].

(A48)

Similarly,
\tilde{\psi}
can be estimated using the held-out contaminated data in
\mathcal{D}^{1}_{b}
:

\displaystyle\tilde{\psi}

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{|\mathcal{D}^{1}_{b}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{D}^{1}_{b}}\mathbb{I}\left[\tilde{Y}_{i}=\hat{f}(X_{i})\right].

(A49)

If the contaminated observations are relatively abundant (i.e.,
|\mathcal{D}^{1}|\gg|\mathcal{D}^{0}|
), one should expect (
A49
) to provide an empirical estimate of
\tilde{\psi}
with low variance compared to that of
\psi
in (
A48
).
Therefore, (
A46
) tells us that the leading source of uncertainty in
\epsilon
is due to
\psi
, which depends on the unknown joint distribution of
(\hat{f}(X),Y)
conditional on the trained classifier
\hat{f}
.
The latter is a multinomial distribution with
K^{2}
categories and event probabilities equal to

\lambda_{lk}=\mathbb{P}\left[\hat{f}(X)=k,Y=l\mid\hat{f}\right],\qquad\forall l,k\in[K].

Then, since
\psi=\sum_{l=1}^{K}\lambda_{ll}
, it is easy to see that
\epsilon
in (
A46
) can be written as a function of the multinomial parameter vector
\lambda=(\lambda_{lk})_{l,k\in[K]}
, as well as of other quantities (
\tilde{\psi}
and
\tilde{\rho}_{k}
) that are already known with relatively high accuracy:

\displaystyle\epsilon=\frac{\sum_{l=1}^{K}\lambda_{ll}-\tilde{\psi}}{\sum_{l=1}^{K}\lambda_{ll}-1/K}.

(A50)

Thus, a confidence interval for
\epsilon
can be directly obtained by applying standard parametric bootstrap techniques for multinomial parameters
[
48
]
, similarly to the more general approach presented in Section
3.3
.
In turn, this immediately translates into a confidence interval
[\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{low}},\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{upp}}]
for
\xi
, which is a monotone increasing function of
\epsilon
, at any desired significance level
\alpha_{V}\in(0,1)
.
Alternatively, one could consider seeking only a point estimate
\hat{\epsilon}
of
\epsilon
, by replacing the multinomial parameters in (
A50
) with their standard maximum-likelihood estimates.

A3.2
The two-level randomized response model

A3.2.1
Model description

This section delves into a different special case of the general label contamination model introduced in Section
2.4
, extending the streamlined methods presented in Section
A3.1
for the randomized response model
[
11
]
to a two-level hierarchical setting.
The model considered here describes a natural label contamination process involving two clearly defined groups of labels.
To illustrate, consider an animal image recognition task: one label group could represent various dog breeds, while the other could denote different cat breeds. The objective is to accurately identify not only the species but also the specific breed for each new image.
In this context, one would often expect that a realistic data annotation process may lead to more frequent mislabeling of dog (or cat) breeds rather than mistakenly identifying a dog as a cat or vice-versa.

The two-level label contamination scenario mentioned above can be formalized using an intuitive model with two scalar parameters:
\epsilon\in[0,1)
and
\nu\in[0,1]
. The parameter
\epsilon
influences the likelihood that the observed label
\tilde{Y}
deviates from the true label
Y
. This mirrors the role of
\epsilon
in the randomized response model outlined in Section
A3.1
. Conversely, the parameter
\nu
governs the interaction between the two distinct label groups.
In the special case of
\nu=0
, this model will reduce to the randomized response model
[
11
]
, indicating an equal probability of mislabeling across breeds or species.
On the other hand, in the special case of
\nu=1
, this model will describe a scenario in which two separate species-specific randomized response models operate independently of one another, and accurate species labeling is always ensured. The most interesting cases will of course be those in between of these two extremes, for values of
\nu\in(0,1)
.
The specifics of this model are presented next.

For simplicity, let us assume that the total number of possible labels,
K
, is even.
We describe the relation between
X,Y
and
\tilde{Y}
with

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\tilde{Y}=k\mid X,Y=l\right]=T_{kl},

(A51)

for all
k,l\in[K]
, where
T\in[0,1]^{K\times K}
is a (
2\times 2
) block matrix defined as

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}D^{\mathrm{BRR}}+B^{\mathrm{BRR}}&C^{\mathrm{BRR}}\\
C^{\mathrm{BRR}}&D^{\mathrm{BRR}}+B^{\mathrm{BRR}}\end{array}\right).

Above, the matrix
D^{\mathrm{BRR}}
is diagonal and such that, for any
k,l\in[K/2]
,

\displaystyle D^{\mathrm{BRR}}_{kl}=(1-\epsilon)\mathbb{I}\left[k=l\right],

while the matrices
B^{\mathrm{BRR}}
and
C^{\mathrm{BRR}}
are constant and such that, for any
k,l\in[K/2]
,

\displaystyle B^{\mathrm{BRR}}_{kl}=\frac{\epsilon}{K}\left(1+\nu\right),

\displaystyle C^{\mathrm{BRR}}_{kl}=\frac{\epsilon}{K}\left(1-\nu\right).

For example, in the special case of
K=4
, the matrix
T
would look like

\displaystyle T^{(4)}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}1-\epsilon+\frac{(1+\nu)\epsilon}{4}&\frac{(1+\nu)\epsilon}{4}&\frac{(1-\nu)\epsilon}{4}&\frac{(1-\nu)\epsilon}{4}\\
\frac{(1+\nu)\epsilon}{4}&1-\epsilon+\frac{(1+\nu)\epsilon}{4}&\frac{(1-\nu)\epsilon}{4}&\frac{(1-\nu)\epsilon}{4}\\
\frac{(1-\nu)\epsilon}{4}&\frac{(1-\nu)\epsilon}{4}&1-\epsilon+\frac{(1+\nu)\epsilon}{4}&\frac{(1+\nu)\epsilon}{4}\\
\frac{(1-\nu)\epsilon}{4}&\frac{(1-\nu)\epsilon}{4}&\frac{(1+\nu)\epsilon}{4}&1-\epsilon+\frac{(1+\nu)\epsilon}{4}\\
\end{array}\right).

It is easy to verify that this two-level randomized response model generally leads to the following contaminated label frequencies:

\displaystyle\tilde{\rho}_{k}

\displaystyle=(1-\epsilon)\rho_{k}+\frac{\epsilon}{K}+\nu\frac{\epsilon}{K}\left(2\sum_{l=1}^{K/2}\rho_{l}-1\right),\qquad\forall k\in[K].

However, we will assume henceforth that
\rho_{k}=1/K
for all
k\in[K]
, which implies
\tilde{\rho}_{k}=1/K
for all
k\in[K]
.
This simplification is not crucial in principle, but it is useful to make the following computations less tedious.
In particular, under the assumption of uniform label frequencies, the matrix
M
in (
1
) is simply equal to
T
:

\displaystyle M=T

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}D^{\mathrm{BRR}}+B^{\mathrm{BRR}}&C^{\mathrm{BRR}}\\
C^{\mathrm{BRR}}&D^{\mathrm{BRR}}+B^{\mathrm{BRR}}\end{array}\right).

Having a relatively simple expression for
M
is useful because it simplifies the implementation of our method, which relies directly on the inverse matrix
V=M^{-1}
.
The latter can now be obtained analytically by combining the Sherman-Morrison formula with standard techniques for block-matrix inversion.
This leads to the following close-formula expressions.
For any
k\in[K]
,

\displaystyle V_{kk}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{1-\epsilon}\left(1-\frac{\epsilon}{K}\right)-\frac{\epsilon\nu}{K(1-\epsilon)\left[1-\epsilon(1-\nu)\right]}.

For any
l\in\mathcal{B}_{k}\setminus\{k\}
, where
\mathcal{B}_{k}
indicates the block to which label
k
belongs—that is,
\mathcal{B}_{k}=\{1,\ldots,K/2\}
if
k\leq K/2
and
\mathcal{B}_{k}=\{K/2+1,\ldots,K\}
otherwise—the term
V_{kl}
is

\displaystyle V_{kl}=-\frac{\epsilon}{K(1-\epsilon)}\cdot\left(1+\frac{\nu}{1-\epsilon(1-\nu)}\right).

Finally, for any
l\in\mathcal{B}_{k}^{\mathrm{c}}
, where
\mathcal{B}_{k}^{\mathrm{c}}:=[K]\setminus\mathcal{B}_{k}
,

\displaystyle V_{kl}=-\frac{\epsilon}{K(1-\epsilon)}\cdot\left(1-\frac{\nu}{1-\epsilon(1-\nu)}\right).

For example, in the special case of
K=4
, the matrix
V
would look like

\displaystyle V^{(4)}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\frac{\epsilon^{2}-\nu\epsilon^{2}+3\nu\epsilon-5\epsilon+4}{4(1-\epsilon)(1-\epsilon+\nu\epsilon)}&-\frac{\epsilon(1-\epsilon+\nu+\nu\epsilon)}{4(1-\epsilon)(1-\epsilon+\nu\epsilon)}&-\frac{(1-\nu)\epsilon}{4(1-\epsilon+\nu\epsilon)}&-\frac{(1-\nu)\epsilon}{4(1-\epsilon+\nu\epsilon)}\\
-\frac{\epsilon(1-\epsilon+\nu+\nu\epsilon)}{4(1-\epsilon)(\nu\epsilon-\epsilon+1)}&\frac{\epsilon^{2}-\nu\epsilon^{2}+3\nu\epsilon-5\epsilon+4}{4(1-\epsilon)(1-\epsilon+\nu\epsilon)}&-\frac{(1-\nu)\epsilon}{4(1-\epsilon+\nu\epsilon)}&-\frac{(1-\nu)\epsilon}{4(1-\epsilon+\nu\epsilon)}\\
-\frac{(1-\nu)\epsilon}{4(1-\epsilon+\nu\epsilon)}&-\frac{(1-\nu)\epsilon}{4(1-\epsilon+\nu\epsilon)}&\frac{\epsilon^{2}-\nu\epsilon^{2}+3\nu\epsilon-5\epsilon+4}{4(1-\epsilon)(1-\epsilon+\nu\epsilon)}&-\frac{\epsilon(1-\epsilon+\nu+\nu\epsilon)}{4(1-\epsilon)(1-\epsilon+\nu\epsilon)}\\
-\frac{(1-\nu)\epsilon}{4(1-\epsilon+\nu\epsilon)}&-\frac{(1-\nu)\epsilon}{4(1-\epsilon+\nu\epsilon)}&-\frac{\epsilon(\nu+\nu\epsilon-\epsilon+1)}{4(1-\epsilon)(1-\epsilon+\nu\epsilon)}&\frac{\epsilon^{2}-\nu\epsilon^{2}+3\nu\epsilon-5\epsilon+4}{4(1-\epsilon)(1-\epsilon+\nu\epsilon)}\\
\end{array}\right).

As detailed below, the relatively tractable structure of the matrix
V
under this two-level randomized response model leads to an easier-to-interpret version of the general methodology presented in Section
3
.
Further, the scalar nature of the unknown parameters
\epsilon\in[0,1)

\nu\in[0,1]
simplifies the task of empirically fitting the model given a limited amount of clean data, thus overcoming the main practical limitation of the general estimation approach described in Section
3.3
.

A3.2.2
Adaptive coverage under a known label contamination model

We begin by showing how the adaptive conformal prediction methodology from Section
3.1
simplifies under a two-level randomized response model for the label contamination process.
First, note that the plug-in estimate
\hat{\Delta}_{k}(t)
of the coverage inflation factor
\Delta_{k}(t)
utilized by Algorithm
1
, originally defined in (
14
), simplifies to:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\Delta}_{k}(t)&=\frac{\epsilon}{1-\epsilon}\left(1-\frac{1}{K}\right)\left[\hat{F}_{k}^{k}(t)-\frac{1}{K-1}\sum_{l\neq k}\hat{F}_{l}^{k}(t)\right]\\
&\qquad-\frac{\epsilon}{1-\epsilon}\cdot\frac{\nu}{1-\epsilon(1-\nu)}\left[\frac{1}{K}\sum_{l\in\mathcal{B}_{k}}\hat{F}_{l}^{k}(t)-\frac{1}{K}\sum_{l\in\mathcal{B}_{k}^{C}}\hat{F}_{l}^{k}(t)\right].\end{split}

(A52)

In the special case of
\nu=0
, this recovers to same expression obtained under the standard randomized response model in (
A39
).
In the other extreme case, if
\nu=1
, the expression for
\hat{\Delta}_{k}(t)
in (
A52
) reduces to

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\Delta}_{k}(t)&=\frac{\epsilon}{1-\epsilon}\left(1-\frac{2}{K}\right)\left[\hat{F}_{k}^{k}(t)-\frac{1}{K/2-1}\sum_{l\in\mathcal{B}_{l}\setminus\{k\}}\hat{F}_{l}^{k}(t)\right],\end{split}

(A53)

consistently with two block-specific randomized response models operating independently of one another.

In the general case, Equation (
A52
) implies that

\displaystyle\sum_{l\neq k}|V_{kl}|

\displaystyle=\frac{\epsilon}{1-\epsilon}\left(1-\frac{1}{K}-\frac{\nu}{K[1-\epsilon(1-\nu)]}\right).

(A54)

Therefore, under a two-level randomized response model, the finite-sample correction term
\delta(n_{k},n_{*})
defined in (
18
) simplifies to

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta(n_{k},n_{*})&=c(n_{k})+\frac{2\epsilon}{(1-\epsilon)\sqrt{n_{*}}}\left(1-\frac{1}{K}-\frac{\nu}{K[1-\epsilon(1-\nu)]}\right)\\
&\qquad\cdot\min\left\{K\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}},\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{*}}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(2K)+\log(n_{*})}{2}}\right\}.\end{split}

Similarly, under this two-level randomized response model, Assumption
4
becomes

\displaystyle\inf_{t\in(0,1)}\Delta_{k}(t)\geq-\alpha+c(n_{k})+\frac{2\epsilon\left(1-\frac{1}{K}-\frac{\nu}{K[1-\epsilon(1-\nu)]}\right)}{(1-\epsilon)\sqrt{n_{*}}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{*}}}+2\sqrt{\frac{\log(2K)+\log(n_{*})}{2}}\right).

This is always satisfied in the limit of
n_{*}\to\infty
, as long as the following inequality holds:

\displaystyle\epsilon\leq\frac{\alpha}{1+\alpha-\frac{1}{K}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\nu}{1-\epsilon(1-\nu)}}.

Instead of solving this quadratic inequality, it suffices here to note that a stricter condition, for any value of
\nu\in[0,1]
, is

\displaystyle\epsilon\leq\frac{\alpha}{2+\alpha-\frac{1}{K}}.

(A55)

Combined with Theorem
3
, these expressions tell us that the prediction sets output by Algorithm
1
have asymptotically tight coverage if the noise parameter
\epsilon
is not too large and the regularity conditions of Assumptions
2
–
3
hold, consistently with the simpler special case of the standard randomized response model (Section
A3.1
).
For example, if
\alpha=0.1
, the upper bound in (
A55
) becomes
\epsilon\leq 0.0625
if
K=2
, and
\epsilon\leq 0.0526
if
K=5
.
Further, also consistently with Section
A3.1
, this model makes it easy to bound from above the term
\varphi_{k}(n_{k},n_{*})
in Theorem
3
, with a bound that only increases with
K
at rate
\sqrt{\log K}
.

A3.2.3
Adaptive coverage under a bounded label contamination model

We now turn our attention to the problem in which the parameters
\epsilon
and
\nu
of the two-level randomized response model are not known exactly.
Under this model, applying the adaptive conformal prediction methods presented in Section
3.2
only requires a simultaneously valid pair of confidence intervals for
\epsilon
and
\nu
, in lieu of a joint confidence region for all off-diagonal elements of
V
.

In particular, if two simultaneously valid confidence intervals
[\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{low}},\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{upp}}]
and
[\hat{\nu}^{\mathrm{low}},\hat{\nu}^{\mathrm{upp}}]
at level
1-\alpha_{V}
are available for
\xi:=\epsilon/(1-\epsilon)
and
\nu
, then it follows immediately that a valid joint confidence region
[\hat{V}^{\mathrm{low}},\hat{V}^{\mathrm{upp}}]
for the off-diagonal elements of
V
is given as follows.

For any
k\in[K]
and
l\in\mathcal{B}_{k}\setminus\{k\}
,

\displaystyle V_{kl}=-\frac{\xi}{K}\cdot\frac{1+\nu(1+2\xi)}{1+\nu\xi}.

It is easy to verify by taking partial derivatives that this is a monotone decreasing function of
\nu
for any fixed
\xi\geq 0
, and a monotone decreasing function of
\xi
for any fixed
\nu\in[0,1]
.
Therefore, for any
k\in[K]
and
l\in\mathcal{B}_{k}\setminus\{k\}
,

\displaystyle\hat{V}^{\mathrm{upp}}_{kl}=-\frac{\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{low}}}{K}\cdot\frac{1+\hat{\nu}^{\mathrm{low}}(1+2\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{low}})}{1+\hat{\nu}^{\mathrm{low}}\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{low}}},

\displaystyle\hat{V}^{\mathrm{low}}_{kl}=-\frac{\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{upp}}}{K}\cdot\frac{1+\hat{\nu}^{\mathrm{upp}}(1+2\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{upp}})}{1+\hat{\nu}^{\mathrm{upp}}\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{upp}}}.

Similarly, for any
k\in[K]
and
l\in\mathcal{B}_{k}^{\mathrm{c}}
,

\displaystyle V_{kl}=-\frac{\xi}{K}\cdot\frac{1-\nu}{1+\nu\xi}.

It is easy to verify by taking partial derivatives that this is a monotone increasing function of
\nu
for any fixed
\xi\geq 0
, and a monotone decreasing function of
\xi
for any fixed
\nu\in[0,1]
.
Therefore, for any
k\in[K]
and
l\in\mathcal{B}_{k}^{\mathrm{c}}
,

\displaystyle\hat{V}^{\mathrm{upp}}_{kl}=-\frac{\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{low}}}{K}\cdot\frac{1-\hat{\nu}^{\mathrm{upp}}}{1+\hat{\nu}^{\mathrm{upp}}\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{low}}},

\displaystyle\hat{V}^{\mathrm{low}}_{kl}=-\frac{\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{upp}}}{K}\cdot\frac{1-\hat{\nu}^{\mathrm{low}}}{1+\hat{\nu}^{\mathrm{low}}\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{upp}}}.

Next, we require an upper confidence bound for the parameter
\hat{\zeta}_{k}
defined in (
20
).
Leveraging the confidence bounds for
\xi
and
\nu
as well as amenable structure of our
V
matrix, we prove in Section
A7.4
that a valid upper bound for
\hat{\zeta}_{k}
is given by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\hat{\zeta}^{\mathrm{upp}}_{k}&=\frac{2}{K}\cdot\frac{\left(\hat{\nu}^{\mathrm{upp}}-\hat{\nu}^{\mathrm{low}}\right)+\left[\hat{\nu}^{\mathrm{upp}}\left(\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{upp}}\right)^{2}-\hat{\nu}^{\mathrm{low}}\left(\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{low}}\right)^{2}\right]+\hat{\nu}^{\mathrm{low}}\hat{\nu}^{\mathrm{upp}}\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{low}}\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{upp}}\left(\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{upp}}-\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{low}}\right)}{\left(1+\hat{\nu}^{\mathrm{upp}}\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{upp}}\right)\left(1+\hat{\nu}^{\mathrm{low}}\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{low}}\right)}\\
&\qquad+\frac{\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{low}}}{K}\cdot\frac{\left(\hat{\nu}^{\mathrm{upp}}-\hat{\nu}^{\mathrm{low}}\right)\left(1+\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{low}}\right)}{\left(1+\hat{\nu}^{\mathrm{low}}\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{low}}\right)\left(1+\hat{\nu}^{\mathrm{upp}}\hat{\xi}^{\mathrm{low}}\right)}.\end{split}

(A56)

Note that, in the special case of
\hat{\nu}^{\mathrm{upp}}=0
and
\hat{\nu}^{\mathrm{low}}=0
, which corresponds to a standard randomized response model with
\nu=0
, we recover that
\hat{\zeta}^{\mathrm{upp}}_{k}=0
, consistently with the results presented earlier in Section
A3.1
.

Leveraging the explicit expressions for
\hat{V}^{\mathrm{low}}
,
\hat{V}^{\mathrm{upp}}
and
\hat{\zeta}^{\mathrm{upp}}_{k}
derived above, one can then directly apply the general conformal prediction methodology detailed in Section
3.2
.
Note that further simplifications of our method’s remaining components within this two-tier randomized response model, though possible in principle, are not explicitly shown here due to the increasingly tedious nature of such analytical calculations beyond this point.
Instead, we will later investigate the performance of our method as a function of the confidence intervals for the parameters
\xi
and
\nu
through numerical experiments.

A3.2.4
Estimating the parameters
\epsilon
and
\nu

Both parameters
\epsilon\in[0,1)
and
\nu\in[0,1)
in the two-level randomized response model described in Section
A3.2.1
can be empirically estimated, from a small amount of clean data, by applying a simplified version of the general procedure described in Section
3.3
.

Recall that the matrices
\tilde{Q}
and
Q
, defined in (
27
), are such that, for any
l,k\in[K]
,

\displaystyle\tilde{Q}_{kl}:=\mathbb{P}\left[\hat{f}(X)=l\mid\tilde{Y}=k,\hat{f}\right],

\displaystyle Q_{kl}:=\mathbb{P}\left[\hat{f}(X)=l\mid Y=k,\hat{f}\right].

Recall also that
\tilde{\psi}
and
\psi
are the probabilities that the classifier guesses correctly the corrupted and true label, respectively, of a new independent data point, as defined in (
A47
).
Then, let us define two new quantities,
\tilde{\phi}
and
\phi
. These are the probabilities that the classifier guesses correctly the group to which the corrupted and true label, respectively, of a new independent data point belong. That is,

\displaystyle\tilde{\phi}:=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{l\in\mathcal{B}_{k}}\mathbb{P}\left[\hat{f}(X)=l,\tilde{Y}=k\mid\hat{f}\right],

\displaystyle\phi:=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{l\in\mathcal{B}_{k}}\mathbb{P}\left[\hat{f}(X)=l,Y=k\mid\hat{f}\right].

We prove in Section
A7.4
that, under the two-level randomized response model, the estimating equation
\tilde{Q}=MQ
stated in (
28
) implies the following system of two equations:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\begin{cases}\tilde{\psi}&=(1-\epsilon)\psi+\frac{\epsilon}{K}+\frac{\epsilon\nu}{K}\left(2\phi-1\right),\\
\tilde{\phi}&=\phi-\epsilon(1-\nu)\left(\phi-\frac{1}{2}\right).\end{cases}\end{split}

(A57)

It is easy to verify that the solution of this system is:

\displaystyle\epsilon=\frac{\frac{K}{2}\left(\psi-\tilde{\psi}\right)-\left(\phi-\tilde{\phi}\right)}{\frac{K}{2}\psi-\phi},

\displaystyle\nu=1-\frac{\left(\phi-\tilde{\phi}\right)\left(\frac{K}{2}\psi-\phi\right)}{\left(\phi-\frac{1}{2}\right)\left[\frac{K}{2}\left(\psi-\tilde{\psi}\right)-\left(\phi-\tilde{\phi}\right)\right]}.

(A58)

This result suggests the following simplified method for estimating
\epsilon
and
\nu
.

The clean data in
\mathcal{D}^{0}
can be used to compute an intuitive empirical estimate of
\psi
using (
A48
), and an estimate of
\phi
as:

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{|\mathcal{D}^{0}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{D}^{0}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{l\in\mathcal{B}_{k}}\mathbb{I}\left[\hat{f}(X_{i})=l,Y_{i}=k\right].

(A59)

Similarly,
\tilde{\psi}
can be estimated using the held-out contaminated data in
\mathcal{D}^{1}_{b}
using (
A49
), and
\tilde{\phi}
can be estimated using

\displaystyle\tilde{\phi}

\displaystyle\approx\frac{1}{|\mathcal{D}^{1}_{b}|}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{D}^{1}_{b}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{l\in\mathcal{B}_{k}}\mathbb{I}\left[\hat{f}(X_{i})=l,\tilde{Y}_{i}=k\right].

(A60)

If the contaminated observations are relatively abundant (i.e.,
|\mathcal{D}^{1}|\gg|\mathcal{D}^{0}|
), one should expect (
A49
) and (
A60
) to provide empirical estimates of
\tilde{\psi}
and
\tilde{\phi}
, respectively, with low variance compared to those of
\psi
and
\phi
.
Therefore, the leading source of uncertainty in
\epsilon
and
\nu
is due to
\psi
and
\phi
, which depend on the unknown joint distribution of
(\hat{f}(X),Y)
conditional on the trained classifier
\hat{f}
.
We already know that the latter is a multinomial distribution with
K^{2}
categories and event probabilities equal to

\lambda_{lk}=\mathbb{P}\left[\hat{f}(X)=k,Y=l\mid\hat{f}\right],\qquad\forall l,k\in[K].

Further,

\displaystyle\psi=\sum_{l=1}^{K}\lambda_{ll},

\displaystyle\phi=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\sum_{l\in\mathcal{B}_{k}}\lambda_{kl}.

(A61)

Therefore, the parameters
\epsilon
and
\nu
in (
A58
) can be written as a function of the multinomial parameter vector
\lambda=(\lambda_{lk})_{l,k\in[K]}
, as well as of other quantities (
\tilde{\psi}
and
\tilde{\phi}
) that are already known with relatively high accuracy.

Thus, confidence intervals for
\epsilon
(or, equivalently,
\xi
) and
\nu
can be directly obtained by applying standard parametric bootstrap techniques for multinomial parameters
[
48
]
, similarly to the case of the standard randomized response model discussed in Section
A3.1
.
Alternatively, one could consider seeking only a point estimate
\hat{\epsilon}
and
\hat{\nu}
, by replacing the multinomial parameters in (
A58
) with their standard maximum-likelihood estimates.

Appendix A4
Comparison to worst-case coverage bounds

A4.1
Theoretical bounds under a general linear contamination model

As previously mentioned in Section
2.4
, it is notable that Theorem
1
could also be utilized to derive worst-case coverage bounds for standard conformal prediction sets calibrated by Algorithm
3
using contaminated data.
This short digression highlights an interesting link with the sophisticated theoretical results of
[
20
]
, although it does not directly add to the methodological advancements presented in this paper.

Corollary 2
.

Suppose
(X_{i},Y_{i},\tilde{Y}_{i})
are i.i.d. for all
i\in[n+1]
, and assume also that Assumption
1
holds.
Fix any prediction function
\mathcal{C}
satisfying Definition
1
, and let
\hat{C}(X_{n+1})
indicate the prediction set output by Algorithm
3
applied using the corrupted labels
\tilde{Y}_{i}
instead of the clean labels
Y_{i}
, for all
i\in[n]
.
Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[Y_{n+1}\in\hat{C}(X_{n+1})\mid Y_{n+1}=k\right]\geq 1-\alpha-\sum_{l\neq k}|V_{kl}|.

(A62)

Further, if the conformity scores
\hat{s}(X_{i},\tilde{Y}_{i})
used by Algorithm
3
are almost-surely distinct,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[Y_{n+1}\in\hat{C}(X_{n+1})\mid Y_{n+1}=k\right]\leq 1-\alpha+\frac{1}{n_{k}+1}+\sum_{l\neq k}|V_{kl}|.

(A63)

The upper and lower coverage bounds established by Corollary
2
are independent of the conformity scores employed by Algorithm
3
, consistently with a worst-case type of analysis.
However, a practical drawback of this generality is the presence of a constant term in the gap between the upper (
A63
) and lower (
A62
) bounds. Therefore, this theoretical worst-case gap cannot vanish asymptotically as the sample size increases:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\text{Worst-Case (WC) Gap}&=\text{WC-UB}-\text{WC-LB}=\frac{1}{n_{k}+1}+2\sum_{l\neq k}|V_{kl}|.\end{split}

(A64)

This is why worst-case coverage bounds such as those provided by Corollary
2
are not as practically relevant for our objectives as the novel methodologies developed in Section
3
.
Indeed, our methods are carefully designed to adaptively learn the unknown distributions of the conformity scores from the existing data.
This allows us to obtain more informative prediction sets with asymptotically tight coverage guarantees, under the assumption that the label contamination process is random as opposed to worst-case.
For example, Theorems
2
and
3
provide coverage lower and upper bounds equal to
1-\alpha
and
1-\alpha+\varphi_{k}(n_{k},n_{*})
, respectively, for the prediction sets output by our Algorithm
1
, where
\varphi_{k}(n_{k},n_{*})
is a known sequence converging to zero as
n_{k}\to\infty
and
n_{*}\to\infty
.

It is important to highlight that the presence of a constant theoretical gap impacts not only the practical utility of our Corollary
2
but also that of the more nuanced theoretical worst-case bounds previously derived by
[
20
]
.
This observation sets the stage for a detailed comparison of these worst-case bounds, which we present in the following subsection along with more detailed evidence of their practical limitations in our context.
For clarity but without much loss of generality, the comparison will be undertaken under the simpler label contamination model previously outlined in Section
A3.1
.

A4.2
Theoretical bounds under a randomized-response model

Assume for simplicity that the label contamination process follows the randomized response model defined in Section
A3.1.1
, with noise parameter
\epsilon\in[0,1)
, and uniform label frequencies
\rho_{k}=1/K=\tilde{\rho}_{k}
for all
k\in[K]
.
Then, it is not difficult to see that the worst-case coverage lower and upper bounds from Corollary
2
become:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{P}\left[Y_{n+1}\in\hat{C}(X_{n+1})\mid Y_{n+1}=k\right]&\geq 1-\alpha-\frac{\epsilon}{1-\epsilon}\left(1-\frac{1}{K}\right),\\
\mathbb{P}\left[Y_{n+1}\in\hat{C}(X_{n+1})\mid Y_{n+1}=k\right]&\leq 1-\alpha+\frac{1}{n_{k}+1}+\frac{\epsilon}{1-\epsilon}\left(1-\frac{1}{K}\right).\end{split}

(A65)

Let us now compare these theoretical bounds with those found by
[
20
]
.
We refer in particular to Appendix C of
[
20
]
, which studies the behavior of standard conformal predictions under a
Huber contamination
model.
Recall that a
\epsilon^{\prime}
-Huber contamination model is a standard model for describing situations in which a fraction
\epsilon^{\prime}\in[0,1)
of data points are contaminated by outliers from an unknown distribution. Thus, our randomized response model with noise parameter
\epsilon
intuitively corresponds to a special case of a Huber contamination model with parameter
\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon(1-1/K)
, because the latter is the expected proportion of contaminated labels
\tilde{Y}
that differ from the true
Y
.
Then, Theorems 2 and 3 in
[
20
]
provide the following worst-case coverage bounds:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{P}\left[Y_{n+1}\in\hat{C}(X_{n+1})\mid Y_{n+1}=k\right]&\geq 1-\alpha-\epsilon\left(1-\frac{1}{K}\right),\\
\mathbb{P}\left[Y_{n+1}\in\hat{C}(X_{n+1})\mid Y_{n+1}=k\right]&\leq 1-\alpha+\frac{1}{n_{k}+1}+\epsilon\left(1-\frac{1}{K}\right).\end{split}

(A66)

These theoretical bounds are similar to ours but tend to be relatively tighter, especially if the label noise parameter is large, because
\epsilon/(1-\epsilon)>\epsilon
for all
\epsilon>0
.
Further, Theorem 6 in
[
20
]
also provides an alternative multiplicative lower bound for the worst-case coverage that can be even tighter than that in (
A66
) if the significance level
\alpha
is small:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[Y_{n+1}\in\hat{C}(X_{n+1})\mid Y_{n+1}=k\right]

\displaystyle\geq 1-\frac{\alpha}{1-\epsilon\left(1-\frac{1}{K}\right)}\approx 1-\alpha-\alpha\epsilon\left(1-\frac{1}{K}\right),

(A67)

where the approximation above holds for small values of
\epsilon
.

In summary, our comparison of (
A65
) with (
A66
) and (
A67
) indicates that a byproduct of Theorem
1
is a worst-case coverage analysis of standard conformal prediction methods in scenarios with label contamination.
This analysis aligns with the more general findings of
[
20
]
, though certainly with less elegance and precision.
It is however worth repeating that the intended function of Theorem
1
is not to conduct a worst-case theoretical analysis of standard conformal prediction methods.
On the contrary, our objective is to develop a new method for adaptively correcting the potential over-coverage or under-coverage of standard conformal predictions, thereby generating more insightful prediction sets.
Theorem
1
, as shown in this paper, paves the path towards effectively accomplishing this goal.

Figure A1
:
Visualization of theoretical worst-case bounds for the coverage achieved by standard conformal prediction sets, calibrated for label-conditional coverage, on the CIFAR-10H data with noisy human-assigned labels. The thick black lines correspond to the theoretical bounds, whereas the light gray line denotes the average empirical coverage achieved by the standard conformal prediction approach in our experiments. The dashed line indicates the nominal 90% coverage level.

In contrast, theoretical worst-case analyses such as those of
[
20
]
are not useful for our aims because they offer no actionable guidance on how to adjust the standard conformal prediction sets.
The fixed and considerable gap between their lower and upper bounds prevents us from understanding how to correct the standard conformal prediction sets in the presence of random label contamination.
As an example, Figure
A1
plots different types of theoretical worst-case upper and lower bounds for the coverage achieved by standard conformal prediction sets, calibrated for label-conditional coverage, on the CIFAR-10H data set studied in Section
4.4
.
See also Figure
A2
for similar results in the context of calibration for marginal instead of label-conditional coverage (see also Section
A5
).
Overall, these results demonstrate that the theoretical bounds may be technically valid but do not provide actionable insight, particularly because they do not even tell us whether the standard conformal prediction sets are too wide or too narrow in the presence of label contamination!

Figure A2
:
Performance of theoretical worst-case bounds for the coverage achieved by standard conformal prediction sets, calibrated for marginal coverage, on the CIFAR-10H data. Other details are as in Figure
A1
.

While the current worst-case analysis suggests that the sole principled approach in the context of label contamination may be to enlarge the standard conformal prediction sets, given that the theoretical lower bounds fall below the nominal
1-\alpha
level, that is not a satisfactory solution.
It lacks adaptability to specific characteristics of the data and classifier, always resulting in prediction sets that are more conservative than the standard ones—which this paper shows already tend to be overly pessimistic.
This issue is exemplified in Figures
A3
and
A4
, which compare the performance of our adaptive method to that of a naive alternative approach inspired by the worst-case theoretical analysis.
The latter simply consists of applying standard conformal prediction methods at a modified significance level
\alpha^{\prime}
defined in such a way that the tightest available (multiplicative) coverage lower bound is exactly equal to the nominal
1-\alpha
coverage level.

As expected, the naive theoretical benchmark in a certain sense worsens rather than mitigates the problem considered in this paper. In fact, it leads to even less informative prediction sets compared to the standard approach ignoring the presence of label contamination.
By contrast, our adaptive method needs no worst-case analysis; instead, leveraging some knowledge of the random label contamination process, it is able to learn from the available data how to correct the standard conformal prediction sets. Thus, our method produces more informative prediction sets that achieve the desired 90% coverage tightly.

Figure A3
:
Performance of the proposed adaptive conformal method on CIFAR-10H image data with noisy human-assigned labels, compared to two benchmarks.
All methods are calibrated to seek 90% label-conditional coverage. The first benchmark is the standard conformal prediction method ignoring the presence of label contamination. The second benchmark is the standard method applied at a more conservative level, chosen such that the best available worst-case theoretical lower coverage bound matches the desired coverage level
1-\alpha
.
Other details are as in Figure
A44
.

Figure A4
:
Performance of the proposed adaptive conformal method on CIFAR-10H image data with noisy human-assigned labels, compared to two benchmarks.
All methods are calibrated to seek 90% marginal coverage.
Other details are as in Figure
1
and Figure
A3
.

Appendix A5
Extensions of preliminary theoretical results

A5.1
General marginal coverage bounds under label contamination

A result similar to Theorem
1
, from Section
2.3
, can be reached about the marginal coverage of the prediction sets produced by Algorithm
4
.
In this case, a useful quantity to define, for any
t\in[0,1]
, is the
marginal coverage inflation factor
,

\displaystyle\Delta(t)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\left[\rho_{k}F_{k}^{k}(t)-\tilde{\rho}_{k}\tilde{F}_{k}^{k}(t)\right]=F(t)-\tilde{F}(t),

(A68)

where
\rho_{k}:=\mathbb{P}[Y=k]
and
\tilde{\rho}_{k}=\mathbb{P}[\tilde{Y}=k]
are the expected proportions of clean and corrupted labels equal to
k
, respectively. Above,
F(t)
and
\tilde{F}(t)
are the marginal cumulative distribution functions of
\hat{s}(X,Y)
and
\hat{s}(X,\tilde{Y})
, respectively, given
\mathcal{D}
; i.e.,

\displaystyle F(t):=\mathbb{P}\left[\hat{s}(X,Y)\leq t\mid\mathcal{D}^{\text{train}}\right],

\displaystyle\tilde{F}(t):=\mathbb{P}\left[\hat{s}(X,\tilde{Y})\leq t\mid\mathcal{D}^{\text{train}}\right].

Theorem A1
.

Suppose
(X_{i},Y_{i},\tilde{Y}_{i})
are i.i.d. for all
i\in[n+1]
.
Fix any prediction function
\mathcal{C}
satisfying Definition
1
, and let
\hat{C}(X_{n+1})
indicate the prediction set output by Algorithm
4
applied using the corrupted labels
\tilde{Y}_{i}
instead of the clean labels
Y_{i}
, for all
i\in[n]
.
Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[Y_{n+1}\in\hat{C}(X_{n+1})\right]\geq 1-\alpha+\mathbb{E}\left[\Delta({\widehat{\tau}})\right].

(A69)

Further, if the scores
\hat{s}(X_{i},\tilde{Y}_{i})
used by Algorithm
4
are almost-surely distinct,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[Y_{n+1}\in\hat{C}(X_{n+1})\right]\leq 1-\alpha+\frac{1}{n_{\mathrm{cal}}+1}+\mathbb{E}\left[\Delta({\widehat{\tau}})\right].

(A70)

A5.2
Coverage lower bounds under a linear contamination model

A similar stochastic dominance condition as in Corollary
1
also implies that the prediction sets output by Algorithm
4
are conservative in the marginal coverage sense of (
5
).

Corollary 3
.

Consider the same setting of Theorem
A1
and assume Assumption
1
holds.
Suppose also that the cumulative distribution functions of the scores (
6
) satisfy

\max_{l\neq k}F_{k}^{l}(t)\leq F_{k}^{k}(t),

(A71)

for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
k\in[K]
.
Then,
\Delta(\hat{\tau})\geq 0
almost-surely, and hence the predictions
\hat{C}(X_{n+1})
of Algorithm
4
satisfy (
5
).

Equation (
A71
) is similar to (
11
), although the two conditions are not exactly equivalent. Intuitively, (
A71
) states that the scores
\hat{s}(X,k)
assigned by the machine learning model tend to be smaller than any other scores
\hat{s}(X,l)
for
l\neq k
among data points with true label
Y=k
. In other words, this could be interpreted as saying that the correct label is the most likely point prediction of the machine learning model, for each possible class
k\in[K]
.

Appendix A6
Methodology extensions

A6.1
Adaptive prediction sets with marginal coverage

While this paper has so far focused on achieving tight label-conditional coverage (
4
), the proposed methods can be adapted to alternatively control the weaker notion of marginal coverage (
5
).
Concretely, we present here Algorithm
7
, which extends for that purpose Algorithm
1
from Section
3.1
.
It easy to see that the analogous extensions of the methods described in Sections
3.2
–
3.3
would also follow similarly.

Algorithm
7
differs from Algorithm
1
in that it calculates a single threshold

\displaystyle\hat{\tau}^{\mathrm{marg}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}S_{(\hat{i}^{\mathrm{marg}})}\text{ where }\hat{i}^{\mathrm{marg}}=\min\{i\in\hat{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathrm{marg}}\},&\text{if }\hat{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathrm{marg}}\neq\emptyset,\\
1,&\text{if }\hat{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathrm{marg}}=\emptyset,\end{cases}

(A72)

where the set
\hat{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathrm{marg}}
is defined as

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathrm{marg}}:=\left\{i\in[n_{\mathrm{cal}}]:\frac{i}{n_{\mathrm{cal}}}\geq 1-\alpha-{\widehat{\Delta}}(S_{(i)})+\delta^{\mathrm{marg}}(n_{\mathrm{cal}},n_{*})\right\},

(A73)

for an empirical estimate
\hat{\Delta}(t)
of the factor
\Delta(t)
in (
A68
) given by

\displaystyle\hat{\Delta}(t)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{k=1}^{K}\left[(\rho_{k}V_{kk}-\tilde{\rho}_{k})\hat{F}_{k}^{k}(t)+\rho_{k}\sum_{l\neq k}V_{kl}\hat{F}_{l}^{k}(t)\right],

(A74)

where, for any
k\in[K]
,

\displaystyle\rho_{k}=\sum_{l=1}^{K}M_{lk}\tilde{\rho}_{l},

(A75)

and a finite-sample correction term taking the form

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta^{\mathrm{marg}}(n_{\mathrm{cal}},n_{*})&:=c(n_{\mathrm{cal}})+\frac{2\max_{k\in[K]}\sum_{l\neq k}|V_{kl}|+\sum_{k=1}^{K}|\rho_{k}-\tilde{\rho}_{k}|}{\sqrt{n_{*}}}\\
&\qquad\cdot\min\left\{K^{2}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{2}},\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{*}}}+\sqrt{\frac{\log(2K^{2})+\log(n_{*})}{2}}\right\}.\end{split}

(A76)

It is worth pointing out that, unlike our adaptive methods for label-conditional coverage presented in Section
3
, Algorithm
7
requires knowledge of the expected class frequencies for the contaminated labels; i.e.,
\tilde{\rho}_{k}
for all
k\in[K]
.
This additional assumption could be relaxed by replacing
\tilde{\rho}_{k}
with an empirical estimate obtained from the available contaminated data, but for simplicity we choose to treat
\tilde{\rho}_{k}
as known. Fortunately, this does not prevent our solution from being practical because
\tilde{\rho}_{k}
is easy to estimate.

1

Input:

Data set
\{(X_{i},\tilde{Y}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
with corrupted labels
\tilde{Y}_{i}\in[K]
.

The inverse
V
of the matrix
M
in (
10
).

The expected contaminated label frequencies
\tilde{\rho}_{k}
, for all
k\in[K]
.

Unlabeled test point with features
X_{n+1}
.

Machine learning algorithm
\mathcal{A}
for training a
K
-class classifier.

Prediction function
\mathcal{C}
satisfying Definition
1
; e.g., (
2
).

Desired coverage level
1-\alpha\in(0,1)
.

Randomly split
[n]
into two disjoint subsets,
\mathcal{D}^{\text{train}}
and
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{cal}}
.

2

Train the classifier
\mathcal{A}
on the data in
\mathcal{D}^{\text{train}}
.

3

Compute conformity scores
\hat{s}(X_{i},k)
using (
1
) for all
i\in\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{cal}}
and all
k\in[K]
.

4

Define the empirical CDF
\hat{F}_{l}^{k}
of
\{\hat{s}(X_{i},k):i\in\mathcal{D}_{l}^{\mathrm{cal}}\}
for all
k,l\in[K]
, as in (
13
).

5

6

Sort
\{\hat{s}(X_{i},Y_{i}):i\in\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{cal}}\}
into
(S_{(1)},S_{(2)},\dots,S_{(n_{\mathrm{cal})}})
, in ascending order.

7

Compute
{\widehat{\Delta}}(S_{(i)})
for all
i\in[n_{\mathrm{cal}}]
, as in (
A74
).

8

Compute
\delta^{\mathrm{marg}}(n_{\mathrm{cal}},n_{*})
using (
A76
), based on a Monte Carlo estimate of
c(n_{\mathrm{cal}})
.

9

\hat{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathrm{marg}}\subseteq[K]
as in (

).

based on
\hat{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathrm{marg}}
as in (

\hat{C}^{\mathrm{marg}}(X_{n+1})=\mathcal{C}(X_{n+1},\hat{\tau}^{\mathrm{marg}};\hat{\pi})
.

Conformal prediction set
\hat{C}^{\mathrm{marg}}(X_{n+1})
for
